\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Coordinates:**
   - Let \((x_1, y_1) = (5, 0)\)
   - Let \((x_2, y_2) = (9, 0)\)

2. **Calculate the Change in Y (\(\Delta y\)):**
   \[
   \Delta y = y_2 - y_1 = 0 - 0 = 0
   \]

3. **Calculate the Change in X (\(\Delta x\)):**
   \[
   \Delta x = x_2 - x_1 = 9 - 5 = 4
   \]

4. **Compute the Slope:**
   \[
   m = \frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x} = \frac{0}{4} = 0
   \]

To solve the problem, we'll follow these steps:

**Step 1: Determine the Slope of the First Line**

The first line passes through the points (0, 8) and (6, 10). The slope \( m \) of a line passing through two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated as:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Plugging in the given points:
\[
m = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line**

If two lines are perpendicular, their slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) satisfy:
\[
m_1 \times m_2 = -1
\]
Given that \( m_1 = \frac{1}{3} \), we can find \( m_2 \):
\[
\frac{1}{3} \times m_2 = -1 \\
m_2 = -3
\]

However, there's a mistake here. The correct calculation should be:
\[
m_2 = -\frac{1}{m_1} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{3}} = -3
\]
But since the slopes of perpendicular lines are negative reciprocals, \( m_2 \) should actually be \( -3 \).

**Step 3: Calculate the Slope of the Second Line**

The second line passes through the points (10, 14) and (\( x \), 26). Using the slope formula:
\[
m = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} = \frac{12}{x - 10}
\]

**Step 4: Set Up the Equation for Perpendicularity**

Since the two lines are perpendicular, their slopes should satisfy:
\[
-\frac{8}{3} \times m_{\text{second line}} = -1
\]
But we have \( m_{\text{second line}} = -3 \), so:
\[
-\frac{8}{3} \times (-3) = 8 \neq -1
\]
This indicates an error in the previous steps. Let's re-evaluate.

**Correct Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Slope of the First Line:**
   \[
   m_1 = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
   \]

2. **Slope of the Perpendicular Line (Negative Reciprocal):**
   \[
   m_2 = -\frac{1}{m_1} = -3
   \]
   
3. **Calculate \( x \) for the Second Line:**
   The slope between (10, 14) and (\( x \), 26) should be equal to \( m_2 = -3 \).
   
   \[
   m_2 = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} = -3 \\
   \frac{12}{x - 10} = -3
   \]
   
   Multiply both sides by \( x - 10 \):
   \[
   12 = -3(x - 10) \\
   12 = -3x + 30 \\
   -3x = 12 - 30 \\
   -3x = -18 \\
   x = \frac{-18}{-3} \\
   x = 6
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{6}
\]

**Solution:**

We need to find a point on the x-axis that is equidistant from the points \( (9, 8) \) and \( (5, 6) \). Given that the x-coordinate of this point is 5, we can represent it as \( P(5, 0) \).

To verify if \( P(5, 0) \) is equidistant from both points, let's calculate the distance from \( P \) to each point using the distance formula:
\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

1. **Distance between \( (5, 0) \) and \( (9, 8) \):**
   \[
   d_1 = \sqrt{(9 - 5)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{4^2 + 8^2} = \sqrt{16 + 64} = \sqrt{80}
   \]

2. **Distance between \( (5, 0) \) and \( (5, 6) \):**
   \[
   d_2 = \sqrt{(5 - 5)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{0 + 36} = \sqrt{36} = 6
   \]

Since both distances are equal (\( \sqrt{80} = 6 \) is not true, but wait—the calculation shows \( d_1 = \sqrt{80} \approx 8.944 \) and \( d_2 = 6 \), which are not equal. However, this contradicts the requirement that \( P(5, 0) \) be equidistant from both points.

**Correction:**

Upon re-evaluating, it's clear there was an error in assuming the x-coordinate is given as 5 without further calculation. To find the correct point on the x-axis equidistant from both points:

Let \( P(x, 0) \) be the desired point.

Set up the equation for distances:
\[
\sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2}
\]

Simplify and solve for \( x \):
\[
(9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36
\]
\[
81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36
\]
\[
145 - 18x = 61 - 10x
\]
\[
-8x = -84 \quad \Rightarrow \quad x = 10.5
\]

So, the correct point is \( (10.5, 0) \).

To determine if the points (4,4), (3,5), and (-1,-1) form a right-angled triangle without using Pythagoras' theorem, we check the slopes of the lines connecting each pair. If any two sides are perpendicular, their slopes will multiply to -1.

1. Calculate slope between (4,4) and (3,5):
   Slope = (5 - 4)/(3 - 4) = 1/(-1) = -1

2. Calculate slope between (3,5) and (-1,-1):
   Slope = (-1 - 5)/(-1 - 3) = (-6)/(-4) = 3/2

3. Calculate slope between (-1,-1) and (4,4):
   Slope = (4 - (-1))/(4 - (-1)) = 5/5 = 1

- (-1)*(1) = -1 → Lines are perpendicular.

The triangle is right-angled with the right angle at (4,4).

I know that angles are typically measured from the x-axis in such problems. To convert the given angle from the y-axis to the x-axis, I subtract 60° from 180°, which gives me 120°. This is the corresponding angle with respect to the positive direction of the x-axis.

Next, I use the slope formula, which involves taking the tangent of this angle. Calculating tan(120°) using trigonometric identities or a calculator provides the slope value.

To find the slope of the line that makes an angle of **60°** with the positive direction of the y-axis measured anticlockwise, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Angle with Respect to the X-Axis
- The given angle (60°) is measured from the positive y-axis.
- To find the corresponding angle with respect to the x-axis:
  
  \[
  \theta = 90^\circ - 60^\circ = 30^\circ
  \]
  
  However, since it's measured anticlockwise from the positive y-axis, which is equivalent to measuring clockwise from the positive x-axis. Therefore, the angle with respect to the x-axis is:

  \[
  \theta = 180^\circ - 60^\circ = 120^\circ
  \]

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope Using the Tangent Function
- The slope \( m \) of a line making an angle \( \theta \) with the positive direction of the x-axis is given by:

  \[
  m = \tan(\theta)
  \]
  
- Substituting \( \theta = 120^\circ \):

  \[
  m = \tan(120^\circ)
  \]

### Step 3: Compute the Tangent of 120°
- Knowing that:

  \[
  \tan(120^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 60^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ)
  \]
  
- And since \( \tan(60^\circ) = \sqrt{3} \):

  \[
  m = -\sqrt{3}
  \]

### Final Answer
The slope of the line is:

\[
\boxed{-\sqrt{3}}
\]

In this case, tanθ is 1/3. So I can set up an equation using that.

Simplifying the numerator: 2m - m is just m. The denominator becomes 1 + 2m². So now I have:

The equation of the line with a tangent of inclination θ equal to 1/6 and an x-intercept at 6 can be derived as follows:

1. **Slope Calculation**: The slope \( m \) is given by \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{6} \).

2. **Point-Slope Form**: Using the x-intercept (6, 0), the equation in point-slope form is:
   \[
   y - 0 = \frac{1}{6}(x - 6)
   \]

3. **Simplifying**:
   \[

   \]

To find the equation of a line that makes intercepts \(-1\) on the \(x\)-axis and \(4\) on the \(y\)-axis, we can use the **intercept form** of the equation of a straight line:

\[

\]

where:
- \(a\) is the x-intercept,
- \(b\) is the y-intercept.

- The x-intercept (\(a\)) is \(-1\),
- The y-intercept (\(b\)) is \(4\),

\[
\frac{x}{-1} + \frac{y}{4} = 1
\]

\[
-\frac{x}{1} + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \\
-x + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \\
\frac{y}{4} = x + 1 \\

Given a point \( R(h, k) \) which divides the line segment from \( (p, 0) \) to \( (0, q) \) in the ratio 1:4, we need to find the equation of the line.

Using the section formula, we know:
- The x-coordinate of \( R \) is given by \( h = \frac{1 \cdot 0 + 4 \cdot p}{1 + 4} = \frac{4p}{5} \). Solving for \( p \), we get \( p = \frac{5h}{4} \).
- The y-coordinate of \( R \) is given by \( k = \frac{1 \cdot q + 4 \cdot 0}{1 + 4} = \frac{q}{5} \). Solving for \( q \), we get \( q = 5k \).

The equation of the line passing through points \( (p, 0) \) and \( (0, q) \) can be written in intercept form as:
\[ \frac{x}{p} + \frac{y}{q} = 1 \]

Substituting \( p = \frac{5h}{4} \) and \( q = 5k \), we get:
\[ \frac{x}{\frac{5h}{4}} + \frac{y}{5k} = 1 \]

Simplifying this, we have:
\[ \frac{4x}{5h} + \frac{y}{5k} = 1 \]

Multiplying through by \( 5hk \) to clear the denominators, we obtain:
\[ 4kx + hy = 5hk \]

Thus, the equation of the line is:
\[
\boxed{4kx + hy = 5hk}
\]

To determine if there are any real lines that pass through the point (4, 4) and have x-intercepts and y-intercepts whose sum is 13, we start by considering the equation of a line in intercept form:

\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]

where \( a \) is the x-intercept and \( b \) is the y-intercept. Given that \( a + b = 13 \), we substitute this into our equation.

Substituting \( b = 13 - a \) into the intercept form:

\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{13 - a} = 1
\]

Since the line passes through (4, 4):

\[
\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{13 - a} = 1
\]

Combining these fractions:

\[
\frac{4(13 - a) + 4a}{a(13 - a)} = 1
\]

Simplifying the numerator:

\[
52 - 4a + 4a = 52
\]

Thus, we have:

\[
\frac{52}{a(13 - a)} = 1 \implies a(13 - a) = 52
\]

This leads to the quadratic equation:

\[
a^2 - 13a + 52 = 0
\]

Calculating the discriminant (\( D \)) for this quadratic equation:

\[
D = (-13)^2 - 4 \times 1 \times 52 = 169 - 208 = -39
\]

Since the discriminant is negative, there are no real solutions for \( a \). Therefore, there are no lines with real intercepts that satisfy both conditions.

**Final Answer:**

There are no such real lines. The solution set is empty.

To find the equation of a line that cuts off equal intercepts on both coordinate axes and passes through the point (4, 5), I'll start by considering the general form of such an equation.

If a line intersects the x-axis at \(a\) and the y-axis at \(-a\), its equation can be written in intercept form as:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{-a} = 1
\]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[
x - y = a
\]

Next, since the line passes through the point (4, 5), I'll substitute \(x = 4\) and \(y = 5\) into the equation to find the value of \(a\):
\[
4 - 5 = a \implies a = -1
\]

With \(a = -1\), the equation of the line becomes:
\[
x - y = -1
\]
Or, rearranged as:
\[
y = x + 1
\]
</think>

To find the equation of a line that cuts off equal intercepts on both coordinate axes and passes through the point \((4, 5)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understanding Equal Intercepts
If a line has equal intercepts on both axes, it means:
- **X-intercept** = \(a\)
- **Y-intercept** = \(-a\) (since they are equal in magnitude but opposite in sign)

The general form of the equation with these intercepts is:
\[
\frac{x}{a} - \frac{y}{a} = 1
\]
Simplifying, we get:
\[
x - y = a
\]

### Step 2: Using the Given Point to Find \(a\)
The line passes through the point \((4, 5)\). Substitute \(x = 4\) and \(y = 5\) into the equation:
\[
4 - 5 = a \\
a = -1
\]

### Step 3: Writing the Final Equation
Substitute \(a = -1\) back into the simplified equation:
\[
x - y = -1 \\
\Rightarrow x - y + 1 = 0 \\
\text{or} \quad y = x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the line is \(\boxed{x - y + 1 = 0}\).

Given a line segment \( AB \) where point \( A \) has coordinates \( (1, 0) \) and point \( B \) has coordinates \( (2, 3) \), we need to determine the set \( S \) such that for each point on \( AB \), the normal line at that point is considered.

1. **Parametrize the line segment \( AB \)**:
   - For any parameter \( t \) where \( 0 \leq t \leq 1 \), the coordinates of a point \( P(t) \) on \( AB \) can be expressed as \( (x, y) = (1 + t, 3t) \).

2. **Find the slope of the normal line**:
   - The slope of \( AB \) is calculated as \( \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = 3 \).
   - The slope of the normal line at any point on \( AB \) is the negative reciprocal, which is \( -\frac{1}{3} \).

3. **Equation of the normal line**:
   - For a point \( P(t) = (1 + t, 3t) \), the equation of the normal line is given by:
     \[
     y - 3t = -\frac{1}{3}(x - (1 + t))
     \]
   - Simplifying this, we get:
     \[
     y = -\frac{1}{3}x + \frac{2}{3} + 4t
     \]

4. **Express the normal line in standard form**:
   - Multiplying through by 3 to clear the fraction:
     \[
     3y = -x + 2 + 12t
     \]
   - Rearranging terms, we get:
     \[
     x + 3y = 2 + 12t
     \]

5. **Determine the range of \( t \)**:
   - As \( t \) varies from 0 to 1, the intercept term changes accordingly:
     - When \( t = 0 \), the equation is \( x + 3y = 2 \).
     - When \( t = 1 \), the equation is \( x + 3y = 14 \).

6. **Final set \( S \)**:
   - The set \( S \) consists of all points that lie on any normal line to \( AB \). Therefore, the region described by these lines is bounded by:
     \[
     2 \leq x + 3y \leq 14
     \]

However, upon re-evaluation, it was realized that the correct intercepts and bounds should be:

- When \( t = 0 \), the normal line equation is \( x + 3y = 1 \).
- When \( t = 1 \), the normal line equation is \( x + 3y = 11 \).

Thus, the set \( S \) is described by the inequality:
\[
1 \leq x + 3y \leq 11
\]

Therefore, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{1 \leq x + 3y \leq 11}
\]

The equation of the line passing through (–5, 7) and perpendicular to the line through points (4, 7) and (-5, 8) has a slope of 9. Using the point-slope form with the given point, the equation is y = 9x + 52.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Slope of the Given Line:**
   - Points on the line are (4,7) and (-5,8).
   - Slope formula: m = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁)
   - Calculation: m = (8 - 7)/(-5 - 4) = 1/(-9) = -1/9.

2. **Determine the Perpendicular Slope:**
   - The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal.
   - Given m₁ = -1/9, the perpendicular slope m₂ = 9 (since (-1/9)*9 = -1).

3. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation:**
   - Using point (-5,7):
     - y - y₁ = m(x - x₁)
     - y - 7 = 9(x +5)

4. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form (y = mx + b):**
   - Distribute the 9: y - 7 = 9x + 45
   - Add 7 to both sides: y = 9x + 52

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the line is {y = 9x + 52}.

To find the equation of the median from vertex \( R \) to side \( PQ \) in triangle \( PQR \), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Coordinates:**
   - Point \( P(2, 1) \)
   - Point \( Q(-2, 3) \)
   - Point \( R(4, 5) \)

2. **Find the Midpoint of Side PQ:**
   
   The midpoint formula is:
   \[
   M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
   \]
   
   Substituting the coordinates of \( P \) and \( Q \):
   \[
   M_x = \frac{2 + (-2)}{2} = 0
   \]
   \[
   M_y = \frac{1 + 3}{2} = 2
   \]
   
   So, the midpoint \( M \) is at \( (0, 2) \).

3. **Determine the Slope of the Median RM:**
   
   The slope formula between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Substituting the coordinates of \( R(4, 5) \) and \( M(0, 2) \):
   \[
   m = \frac{5 - 2}{4 - 0} = \frac{3}{4}
   \]

4. **Write the Equation Using Point-Slope Form:**
   
   The point-slope form of a line is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Substituting \( R(4, 5) \) and slope \( \frac{3}{4} \):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{3}{4}(x - 4)
   \]
   
   Simplify to get the equation in standard form:
   \[
   4(y - 5) = 3(x - 4)
   \]
   \[
   4y - 20 = 3x - 12
   \]
   \[
   3x - 4y + 8 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{3x - 4y + 8 = 0}

The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x – 6y –28 = 0 is calculated using the formula for distance from a point to a line. The result is \(\frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}\) or \(\frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61}\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the values:** From the equation 5x - 6y -28 = 0, A=5, B=-6, C=-28.
2. **Point coordinates:** (5, -7) → x=5, y=-7.
3. **Calculate numerator:** |(5*5) + (-6*-7) + (-28)| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39| = 39.
4. **Calculate denominator:** sqrt(A² + B²) = sqrt(25 + 36) = sqrt(61).
5. **Distance formula:** 39 / sqrt(61). Rationalizing, it becomes (39√61)/61.

Given points \( A(4, 5) \), \( B(6, 1) \), and \( C(3, 4) \), we need to find the length of the altitude from point \( A \) to side \( BC \).

1. **Check if triangle ABC is right-angled:**
   - Calculate vectors \( \overrightarrow{AC} \) and \( \overrightarrow{BC} \):
     - \( \overrightarrow{AC} = (3 - 4, 4 - 5) = (-1, -1) \)
     - \( \overrightarrow{BC} = (3 - 6, 4 - 1) = (-3, 3) \)
   - Compute the dot product of \( \overrightarrow{AC} \) and \( \overrightarrow{BC} \):
     - Dot product: \( (-1)(-3) + (-1)(3) = 3 - 3 = 0 \)
   - Since the dot product is zero, vectors are perpendicular. Hence, triangle \( ABC \) is right-angled at \( C \).

2. **Determine the length of the altitude from \( A \):**
   - In a right-angled triangle, one leg serves as the altitude for the hypotenuse.
   - Calculate the distance between points \( A(4, 5) \) and \( C(3, 4) \):
     - Distance formula: \( \sqrt{(4 - 3)^2 + (5 - 4)^2} = \sqrt{1^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{2} \)

Thus, the length of the altitude from point \( A \) to side \( BC \) is \(\boxed{\sqrt{2}}\).

To find the **distance between two parallel lines**, we'll follow these steps:

1. **Ensure Both Lines Are in Standard Form**:
   - The first line is already in standard form: \( 19x + 12y - 38 = 0 \).
   - The second line can be simplified by dividing all terms by 2 to make it comparable with the first line:
     \[

     19x + 12y + 35 = 0
     \]

   - The constants from each line are:
     - First Line: \( C_1 = -38 \)
     - Second Line: \( C_2 = 35 \)

   \[
   d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]

   \[

   d = \frac{|-73|}{\sqrt{361 + 144}} \\
   d = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}} \\

The distance from the point (-2, 2) to the line 24(x + 12) = 10(y – 4) is calculated using the formula for the distance from a point (x₀, y₀) to a line Ax + By + C = 0. First, rewrite the given equation in standard form:

Starting with:
\[ 24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4) \]
Expanding both sides:
\[ 24x + 288 = 10y - 40 \]
Bringing all terms to one side:
\[ 24x - 10y + 328 = 0 \]

Here, \( A = 24 \), \( B = -10 \), and \( C = 328 \).

Using the distance formula:
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]
Substituting \( (x₀, y₀) = (-2, 2) \):
\[
\text{Numerator} = |24(-2) + (-10)(2) + 328| = |-48 -20 + 328| = |260|
\]
\[
\text{Denominator} = \sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2} = \sqrt{576 + 100} = \sqrt{676} = 26
\]
Thus, the distance is:
\[
\frac{260}{26} = 10
\]

To find the intercepts of the equation \( 12x - 9y = 18 \) in its intercept form, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Convert to Intercept Form
The intercept form of a linear equation is:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]
where \( a \) is the x-intercept and \( b \) is the y-intercept.

Start by dividing both sides of the given equation by 18 to simplify it:
\[
\frac{12x}{18} - \frac{9y}{18} = \frac{18}{18}
\]
Simplify the fractions:
\[
\frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1
\]
Rewrite to match the intercept form:
\[
\frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1
\]

### Step 2: Identify Intercepts

- **X-intercept (\( a \))**: The denominator under \( x \) is \( \frac{3}{2} \), so:
  \[

  \]

\[

\]

The distance between (1, 2) and \(\left(-\frac{5}{18}, -\frac{5}{9}\right)\) is:
\[

\]

The equation of the line midway between \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\) and its parallel counterpart is found by scaling both lines to have identical coefficients for x and y, then averaging their constants. This results in the midpoint line having a constant term which is the average of the scaled constants from the original lines.

**Step-by-step Explanation:**

1. **Express Both Lines with Same Coefficients:**
   - Original Line 1: \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\)
   - Multiply by 3 to match coefficients:
     \(9x + 6y + 18 = 0\)

2. **Find the Midpoint Constant:**
   - The other line (Line 2) is \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\) after scaling.
   - Average constants: \(\frac{-7 + 18}{2} = \frac{11}{2}\)

3. **Form the Midline Equation:**
   - Combine to get \(9x + 6y + \frac{11}{2} = 0\)
   - Multiply by 2 to eliminate fraction:
     \(18x + 12y + 11 = 0\)

**Answer:**  
The midpoint line equation is \(\boxed{18x + 12y + 11 = 0}\).

= (8 - (-4)) / (-2 - 6)

Denominator: -2 - 6 = -8

To find the slope of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\), we use the slope formula:

\[
\text{Slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the coordinates:**
   \[
   (x_1, y_1) = (6, -4)
   \]
   \[
   (x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)
   \]

2. **Plug the values into the slope formula:**
   \[
   \text{Slope} = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6}
   \]

3. **Calculate the numerator and denominator:**
   \[
   8 - (-4) = 12
   \]
   \[
   -2 - 6 = -8
   \]

4. **Simplify the slope:**
   \[
   \text{Slope} = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{3}{2}}
\]

To determine the slope of a line inclined at 120 degrees from the positive x-axis, I can use trigonometry.

First, calculate the tangent of the given angle to find the slope. Since tan(θ) equals the slope (m), substitute θ with 120 degrees.

Next, apply the reference angle concept because 120 degrees is in the second quadrant where the tangent function yields a negative value. The reference angle for 120 degrees is found by subtracting it from 180 degrees, resulting in 60 degrees.

Finally, calculate tan(60°), which equals √3. Incorporating the sign based on the quadrant, the slope becomes -√3.
</think>

To find the slope of a line that makes an inclination of \(120^\circ\) with the positive direction of the x-axis, we can use trigonometry.

**Step 1: Understand the Relationship Between Slope and Angle**

The slope (\(m\)) of a line is related to its angle of inclination (\(\theta\)) by the formula:

\[ m = \tan(\theta) \]

where \(0^\circ < \theta < 180^\circ\) for lines in standard position.

**Step 2: Calculate the Tangent of the Given Angle**

Given that \(\theta = 120^\circ\):

\[
m = \tan(120^\circ)
\]

**Step 3: Simplify Using Reference Angles**

Since \(120^\circ\) is in the second quadrant, we can find its reference angle by subtracting from \(180^\circ\):

\[
\text{Reference Angle} = 180^\circ - 120^\circ = 60^\circ
\]

In the second quadrant, tangent values are negative:

\[
\tan(120^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ)
\]

**Step 4: Calculate \(\tan(60^\circ)\)**

We know that:

\[
\tan(60^\circ) = \sqrt{3}
\]

Therefore:

\[
m = -\sqrt{3}
\]

**Final Answer**

The slope of the line is:

The problem involves finding the slope of a line when given that the angle between it and another line with slope \( \frac{1}{4} \) is \( \frac{\pi}{4} \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Formula for Angle Between Two Lines:**
   The tangent of the angle θ between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is given by:
   \[
   \tan\theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
   \]
   
2. **Given Values:**
   Here, θ = \( \frac{\pi}{4} \) and \( m_1 = \frac{1}{4} \). So,
   \[
   \tan\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} \right|
   \]

3. **Removing Absolute Value:**
   This leads to two possible equations:
   \[
   \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = 1 \quad \text{(Case 1)}
   \]
   \[
   \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = -1 \quad \text{(Case 2)}
   \]

4. **Solving Case 1:**
   Multiply both sides by the denominator:
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2
   \]
   
   Rearranging terms:
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{1}{4} m_2 = 1 + \frac{1}{4}
   \]
   \[
   \frac{3}{4} m_2 = \frac{5}{4}
   \]
   Multiply both sides by \( \frac{4}{3} \):
   \[
   m_2 = \frac{5}{3}
   \]

5. **Solving Case 2:**
   Multiply both sides by the denominator:
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = -1 - \frac{1}{4} m_2
   \]
   
   Rearranging terms:
   \[
   m_2 + \frac{1}{4} m_2 = -1 + \frac{1}{4}
   \]
   \[
   \frac{5}{4} m_2 = -\frac{3}{4}
   \]
   Multiply both sides by \( \frac{4}{5} \):
   \[
   m_2 = -\frac{3}{5}
   \]

6. **Verification:**
   Both slopes satisfy the original condition, as verified by plugging them back into the tangent formula.

**Final Answer:** The possible slopes are \( \frac{5}{3} \) and \( -\frac{3}{5} \).

To determine the value of \( x \), let's follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope of the first line (through points \( (0, 8) \) and \( (6, 10) \)):**

   The formula for slope (\( m \)) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the values:
   \[
   m_1 = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
   \]

2. **Determine the slope of the second line (perpendicular to the first):**

   Two perpendicular lines have slopes that are negative reciprocals of each other:
   \[
   m_2 = -\frac{1}{m_1} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{3}} = -3
   \]

3. **Calculate \( x \) using the slope formula for the second line (through points \( (10, 14) \) and \( (x, 26) \)):**

   Using the slope formula:
   \[

   \[

   \]
   \[

   \]
   \[

To find the area of the quadrilateral with vertices (–6,7), (2,9), (7, –3), and (–2,0), we use the shoelace formula. 

The shoelace formula for a polygon with vertices \((x_1, y_1), (x_2, y_2), \ldots, (x_n, y_n)\) is given by:
\[
\text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \left| \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} (x_i y_{i+1}) + x_n y_1 - \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} (y_i x_{i+1}) - y_n x_1 \right|
\]

Applying this to the given vertices in order:

1. Calculate \( \sum (x_i y_{i+1}) \):
   \[
   (-6) \cdot 9 + 2 \cdot (-3) + 7 \cdot 0 + (-2) \cdot 7 = -54 - 6 + 0 - 14 = -74
   \]

2. Calculate \( \sum (y_i x_{i+1}) \):
   \[
   7 \cdot 2 + 9 \cdot 7 + (-3) \cdot (-2) + 0 \cdot (-6) = 14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83
   \]

3. Compute the absolute difference and divide by 2:
   \[
   \left| -74 - 83 \right| / 2 = \left| -157 \right| / 2 = 157/2
   \]

Thus, the area of the quadrilateral is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{157}{2}}
\]

To find a point on the **x-axis** that is equidistant from the points \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\), and has an \(x\)-coordinate of \(5\), follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   - We need to find a point on the x-axis with an \(x\)-coordinate of \(5\).
   - This point should be equidistant from \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\).

2. **Determine the Point on the X-Axis:**
   - Any point on the x-axis has coordinates \((x, 0)\).
   - Given \(x = 5\), the point is \((5, 0)\).

3. **Verify Equidistance Using Distance Formula:**
   - The distance between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
     \[
     d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
     \]
   
   - **Distance from \((5, 0)\) to \((9, 8)\):**
     \[
     d_1 = \sqrt{(9 - 5)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{4^2 + 8^2} = \sqrt{16 + 64} = \sqrt{80} \approx 8.94
     \]
   
   - **Distance from \((5, 0)\) to \((5, 6)\):**
     \[
     d_2 = \sqrt{(5 - 5)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{0^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{36} = 6
     \]
   
   - **Conclusion:**
     Since \(d_1\) and \(d_2\) are not equal, there is no such point on the x-axis with an \(x\)-coordinate of \(5\) that satisfies the equidistance condition.

However, if we consider finding a different point on the x-axis where the distances to both \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\) are equal (without fixing the \(x\)-coordinate), here's how you would approach it:

1. **Let the Point be \((h, 0)\).**
2. **Set Up the Distance Equations:**
   - Distance from \((h, 0)\) to \((9, 8)\):
     \[
     d_1 = \sqrt{(h - 9)^2 + (0 - 8)^2}
     \]
   
   - Distance from \((h, 0)\) to \((5, 6)\):
     \[
     d_2 = \sqrt{(h - 5)^2 + (0 - 6)^2}
     \]
   
3. **Set the Distances Equal:**
   \[
   \sqrt{(h - 9)^2 + (-8)^2} = \sqrt{(h - 5)^2 + (-6)^2}
   \]
   
4. **Square Both Sides to Eliminate Square Roots:**
   \[
   (h - 9)^2 + 64 = (h - 5)^2 + 36
   \]
   
5. **Expand and Simplify:**
   \[
   h^2 - 18h + 81 + 64 = h^2 - 10h + 25 + 36
   \]
   \[
   -18h + 145 = -10h + 61
   \]
   
6. **Solve for \(h\):**
   \[
   -18h + 145 = -10h + 61 \\
   -8h = -84 \\
   h = 10.5
   \]
   
7. **Final Answer:**
   The point on the x-axis that is equidistant from both \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\) is:
   \[
   \boxed{10.5}
   \]

To determine the slope of a line that passes through the origin and the midpoint of the segment joining points \( P(2, -2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint
The midpoint \( M \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is calculated using:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the coordinates of points \( P \) and \( B \):
\[
M_x = \frac{2 + 10}{2} = 6
\]
\[
M_y = \frac{-2 + 2}{2} = 0
\]
So, the midpoint is:
\[
M(6,\ 0)
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope
The slope \( m \) of a line passing through two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Here, the two points are the origin \( O(0,\ 0) \) and the midpoint \( M(6,\ 0) \):
\[
m = \frac{0 - 0}{6 - 0} = \frac{0}{6} = 0
\]

### Final Answer:
The slope of the line is \(\boxed{0}\).

To determine if the points \( A = (4, 4) \), \( B = (3, 5) \), and \( C = (-1, -1) \) form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem, I can use vector analysis by checking if any two sides are perpendicular.

First, calculate the vectors representing each side:
- Vector \( AB \): From point A to point B is \( (3 - 4, 5 - 4) = (-1, 1) \)
- Vector \( AC \): From point A to point C is \( (-1 - 4, -1 - 4) = (-5, -5) \)
- Vector \( BC \): From point B to point C is \( (-1 - 3, -1 - 5) = (-4, -6) \)

Next, check the dot product of each pair of vectors:
1. Dot product of \( AB \) and \( AC \):
   \[
   (-1)(-5) + (1)(-5) = 5 - 5 = 0
   \]
   Since the dot product is zero, vectors \( AB \) and \( AC \) are perpendicular.

Since one pair of sides forms a right angle at point A, the triangle is indeed right-angled.

To determine if the points \( A = (4, 4) \), \( B = (3, 5) \), and \( C = (-1, -1) \) form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem, we can use vector analysis by checking if any two sides are perpendicular.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Calculate Vectors:**
   - **Vector AB:** From point A to point B.
     \[
     \overrightarrow{AB} = (3 - 4, 5 - 4) = (-1, 1)
     \]
   - **Vector AC:** From point A to point C.
     \[
     \overrightarrow{AC} = (-1 - 4, -1 - 4) = (-5, -5)
     \]
   - **Vector BC:** From point B to point C.
     \[
     \overrightarrow{BC} = (-1 - 3, -1 - 5) = (-4, -6)
     \]

2. **Check for Perpendicularity:**
   Two vectors are perpendicular if their dot product is zero.

   - **Dot Product of AB and AC:**
     \[
     \overrightarrow{AB} \cdot \overrightarrow{AC} = (-1)(-5) + (1)(-5) = 5 - 5 = 0
     \]
     Since the dot product is zero, vectors \( \overrightarrow{AB} \) and \( \overrightarrow{AC} \) are perpendicular.

### Conclusion:
Since at least one pair of sides (\( AB \) and \( AC \)) forms a right angle (their dot product is zero), the triangle formed by points A, B, and C is indeed a **right-angled** triangle.

Yes, so theta_x = 150°. Therefore, m = tan(150°).

Now compute tan(150°). Since 150 degrees is in the second quadrant where tangent is negative.

We can write this as:

tan(180 - 30) = -tan(30)

And since tan(30) is √3/3, then tan(150) = -√3/3 ≈ -0.577.

So the slope m would be -√3/3.
</think>

The angle made by the line with the positive y-axis (60°) translates to an angle of 150° from the positive x-axis in standard position. The slope is calculated as tan(150°), which equals -√3⁄3.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Determine theta_x:**
   - Since the line makes a 60° angle with the positive y-axis, measured anticlockwise.
   - In standard coordinate system terms, this corresponds to an angle of 90° + 60° = 150° from the positive x-axis.

2. **Calculate slope (m):**
   - m = tan(theta_x) = tan(150°).
   - Since 150° is in the second quadrant where tangent is negative.
   - tan(150°) = tan(180° - 30°) = -tan(30°).

3. **Evaluate tan(30°):**
   - tan(30°) = √3⁄3.

4. **Final slope:**
   - m = -√3⁄3 ≈ -0.577.

Answer:
$\boxed{-\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{3}}$

To find the angle between the x-axis and the line joining the points \( (5, 1) \) and \( (6, 0) \), follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope (\( m \)) of the Line:**

   The slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]

   Plugging in the coordinates:
   \[

   \]

2. **Determine the Angle (\( \theta \)) with Respect to the x-Axis:**

   The angle \( \theta \) can be found using the arctangent function:
   \[

   \]
   \[

   \]

3. **Calculate the Angle in Degrees:**

   Since \( \arctan(1) = 45^\circ \), then:
   \[

   \]

Similarly, if a line is parallel to the y-axis, its x-coordinate remains constant. Given that the point has an x-coordinate of 0, the equation of this vertical line would be x = 0.
</think>

To find the equations of the lines parallel to both axes and passing through the point \((0, 5)\), we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Requirements
- **Line parallel to the x-axis:** This is a horizontal line where all points have the same y-coordinate.
- **Line parallel to the y-axis:** This is a vertical line where all points have the same x-coordinate.

### Step 2: Determine the Equations

   \[

   - Since the point is \((0, 5)\), the x-coordinate (\(x\)) is 0.
   
   Therefore, the equation is:
   \[

Same as before! So yes, all three points are on the same line. I'm confident now.

The section formula states that if a point divides a line segment joining (x₁, y₁) and (x₂, y₂) in the ratio m:n internally, then the coordinates of the point are:

\[

\]

In this case, our points A and B are (a, 0) and (0, b), respectively. The ratio is 1:4, so m = 1 and n = 4.

Given that \( h \) and \( k \) are real numbers such that the point \( R(h, k) \) lies on the line segment \( AB \), we need to find the equation of the line \( AB \).

1. **Identify coordinates of points \( A \) and \( B \)**:
   - Since \( R(h, k) \) divides \( AB \) in the ratio 1:4, point \( A \) is at \( (5h/4, 0) \) and point \( B \) is at \( (0, 5k) \).

2. **Calculate the slope of line \( AB \)**:
   - The slope \( m \) between points \( A(5h/4, 0) \) and \( B(0, 5k) \) is given by:
     \[
     m = \frac{5k - 0}{0 - 5h/4} = \frac{5k}{-5h/4} = -\frac{4k}{h}
     \]

3. **Equation of line \( AB \)**:
   - Using the point-slope form with point \( B(0, 5k) \):
     \[
     y - 5k = -\frac{4k}{h}(x - 0)
     \]
   - Simplifying this, we get:
     \[
     y = -\frac{4k}{h}x + 5k
     \]

4. **Convert to standard form**:
   - Multiply through by \( h \) to eliminate the fraction:
     \[
     hy = -4kx + 5hk
     \]
   - Rearrange terms to get the standard form:
     \[
     4kx + hy = 5hk
     \]

Thus, the equation of line \( AB \) is \(\boxed{4xk + hy = 5hk}\).

Given two points (x1,y1) and (x2,y2), the slope m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1).

m = (1320 - 1080)/(116 - 114) = 240/2 = 120.

y - 1080 = 120*(x - 114)

y = 120x - 120*114 + 1080

Thus, at x=117:

y=120*117 -12600

Calculating again: 120*117 is indeed 14040; minus 12600 gives 1440. So that's consistent.

So, despite seeming counterintuitive (higher price leading to higher quantity sold), the math checks out.

The equation of the line can be found using the point-slope formula with the given perpendicular point and calculating the negative reciprocal slope.

**Step-by-step Explanation:**

1. **Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line:**
   - The perpendicular from the origin passes through (2, 13).
   - Calculate its slope: \( \frac{13 - 0}{2 - 0} = \frac{13}{2} \).

2. **Find the Original Line's Slope:**
   - Since they are perpendicular, the original line’s slope is the negative reciprocal of \( \frac{13}{2} \), which is \( -\frac{2}{13} \).

3. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation:**
   - Using point (2, 13) and slope \( -\frac{2}{13} \):
     \( y - 13 = -\frac{2}{13}(x - 2) \).
   
4. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form:**
   - Distribute the slope:
     \( y - 13 = -\frac{2}{13}x + \frac{4}{13} \).
   - Add 13 to both sides (expressed as \( \frac{169}{13} \) for common denominators):
     \( y = -\frac{2}{13}x + \frac{4}{13} + \frac{169}{13} \).
   - Combine constants:
     \( y = -\frac{2}{13}x + \frac{173}{13} \).

5. **Express in Standard Form (Optional):**
   - Multiply through by 13: \( 13y = -2x + 173 \).
   - Rearrange: \( 2x + 13y = 173 \).

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the line is \( y = -\frac{2}{13}x + \frac{173}{13} \) or in standard form, \( 2x + 13y = 173 \).

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a line that goes through the point (0,2) and makes an angle of 2π/3 with the positive x-axis. Then, I have to find another line parallel to this one but crossing the y-axis at a distance of 2 units below the origin.

First, let's tackle the first part. The angle given is 2π/3 radians. Since π radians is 180 degrees, 2π/3 would be (2/3)*180 = 120 degrees. So the line makes a 120-degree angle with the positive x-axis. That means it's sloping upwards to the left.

The slope of a line is given by the tangent of the angle it makes with the x-axis. So, slope m = tan(θ). Here, θ is 2π/3, which is 120 degrees. The tangent of 120 degrees can be found using the unit circle or trigonometric identities.

I remember that tan(180° - θ) = -tanθ. So tan(120°) = tan(180° - 60°) = -tan(60°). The value of tan(60°) is √3, so tan(120°) should be -√3. Therefore, the slope m = -√3.

Now that I have the slope, and knowing the line passes through (0,2), which is actually the y-intercept because it's on the y-axis at x=0. The equation of a line in slope-intercept form is y = mx + b, where m is the slope and b is the y-intercept.

Substituting the values I have: m = -√3 and b = 2 (since when x=0, y=2). So the equation should be y = -√3 x + 2. That's straightforward.

Now moving on to the second part: finding a line parallel to this one that crosses the y-axis at a distance of 2 units below the origin. Since it's parallel, it must have the same slope as the first line, which is m = -√3.

The y-intercept for the parallel line needs to be 2 units below the origin. The origin is (0,0), so moving down 2 units along the y-axis would place the point at (0,-2). Therefore, the y-intercept b' for this new line should be -2.

Using the same slope and the new y-intercept, the equation of the second line would also be in the form y = mx + b'. Substituting the values: m = -√3 and b' = -2. So the equation is y = -√3 x - 2.

Wait a minute, let me double-check that. The slope should remain the same because it's parallel, yes. And since it crosses the y-axis below the origin by 2 units, starting at (0,-2), so b' is indeed -2. That seems right.

So summarizing:
1. First line: slope m = -√3, passing through (0,2) gives equation y = -√3 x + 2.
2. Second line: same slope m = -√3, but crossing the y-axis at (0,-2), so equation is y = -√3 x - 2.

I think that's it. I should probably write these equations in standard form as well to present them neatly. Let me convert both into Ax + By + C = 0 form.

For the first line:
y = -√3 x + 2
Adding √3 x to both sides: √3 x + y - 2 = 0

For the second line:
y = -√3 x - 2
Adding √3 x and adding 2 to both sides: √3 x + y + 2 = 0

But maybe in the final answer, it's okay to present them as slope-intercept form since they asked for equations of lines. I think either form is acceptable unless specified otherwise.

Alternatively, if needed in standard form, but perhaps not necessary here.
</think>

The equation of the line through (0,2) with a slope determined by an angle of 120° from the x-axis is found using m = tan(120°) = -√3. Thus, the equation is y = -√3 x + 2.

For the parallel line crossing the y-axis at (0,-2), the same slope applies: m = -√3. Hence, its equation is y = -√3 x - 2.

**Final Answer:**  
The equations are {y = -√3 x + 2} and {y = -√3 x - 2}.

**Final Answer**

The problem asks for a line passing through the point \((4, 4)\) whose sum of intercepts is 13. 

First, we represent the line in its intercept form: \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1\), where \(a\) and \(b\) are the x-intercept and y-intercept respectively. The line passes through \((4, 4)\), so substituting this point into the equation gives:

\[
\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{b} = 1
\]

We also know that the sum of the intercepts is 13:

\[
a + b = 13
\]

From the first equation, we can rewrite it as:

\[
\frac{4(1/a + 1/b)}{1} = 1 \implies \frac{1}{a} + \frac{1}{b} = \frac{1}{4}
\]

We now have two equations:
1. \(a + b = 13\)
2. \(\frac{1}{a} + \frac{1}{b} = \frac{1}{4}\)

Rewriting the second equation using a common denominator:

\[
\frac{a + b}{ab} = \frac{1}{4}
\]

Substituting \(a + b = 13\) into the equation:

\[
\frac{13}{ab} = \frac{1}{4} \implies ab = 52
\]

We now have the system of equations:
1. \(a + b = 13\)
2. \(ab = 52\)

This can be represented as a quadratic equation:

\[
t^2 - 13t + 52 = 0
\]

Calculating the discriminant:

\[
D = (-13)^2 - 4 \cdot 1 \cdot 52 = 169 - 208 = -39
\]

Since the discriminant is negative, there are no real solutions for \(a\) and \(b\). Therefore, such a line does not exist in the real plane.

\[
\boxed{\text{No such line exists}}
\]

Second case: 4/(-a) +5/a=1 ⇒ (-4 +5)/a=1 ⇒1/a=1⇒a=1. Therefore, the equation becomes x/(-1)+y/1 =1 ⇒-x + y=1 ⇒y=x+1.

So yes, there are two lines: one with both intercepts positive (a=9) and another where they're of opposite signs but equal magnitude (a=1 for negative side).

Hence, the conclusion is that these are the two solutions.

Therefore, I think it's correct to provide both equations as answers.

If "equal lengths", then direction doesn't matter, so magnitude is considered. So yes, including the cases where one is positive and other negative.

Hence, there are two possible lines: x + y =9 and x - y =-1 (which is same as y=x+1).

Therefore, the answer would include both these equations.

To determine the equations of the lines that pass through the point \((4, 5)\) and cut off equal lengths on both axes, we consider two cases:

1. **Both intercepts are positive**: The equation is \(x/a + y/a = 1\). Substituting the point:
   \[
   \frac{4}{a} + \frac{5}{a} = 1 \implies \frac{9}{a} = 1 \implies a = 9
   \]
   Thus, the equation is \(x + y = 9\).

2. **One intercept is positive and the other is negative**: The equation is \(x/(-a) + y/a = 1\). Substituting the point:
   \[
   -\frac{4}{a} + \frac{5}{a} = 1 \implies \frac{1}{a} = 1 \implies a = 1
   \]
   Thus, the equation is \(x - y = -1\) or \(y = x + 1\).

**Answer:** The equations of the lines are \(\boxed{x + y = 9}\) and \(\boxed{x - y = -1}\).

The section formula says that if a point P divides the line segment joining points A(x₁,y₁) and B(x₂,y₂) in the ratio m:k, then the coordinates of P are ((mx₂ + kx₁)/(m + k), (my₂ + ky₁)/(m + k)).

x = (1*2 + n*1)/(1 + n)
y = (1*3 + n*0)/(1 + n)

Simplifying that, x becomes (2 + n)/(1 + n) and y is 3/(1 + n). So point P is ((n+2)/(n+1), 3/(n+1)).

Wait perhaps if curve is a circle:

Equation of circle: (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

Tangent from external point A(-2,0) with slope m=-1/3; then equation of tangent line can be given as y = (-1/3)(x + 2).

Condition for this line to be tangent is that the distance from center (h,k) to the line equals radius.

Formula: |A*h + B*k + C| / sqrt(A² + B²)=r

Here, equation of line: y= (-1/3)x - 2/3; rearranged: (1/3)x + y + 2/3 =0 ⇒ Multiply both sides by 3 to eliminate fractions:

x + 3y + 2=0.

So distance from center (h,k) is |h + 3k + 2| / sqrt(1²+3²)= |h +3k +2| / sqrt(10)

Similarly for tangent from B: line y = (-1/3)(x -3). So equation in standard form:

(-1/3)x - y +1=0 ⇒ Multiply by 3: x + 3y -3=0.

Distance from center (h,k) to this line is |h +3k -3| / sqrt(10)=r

Also, both distances should equal radius r. So:

|h +3k +2| = |h +3k -3|

Which implies either h +3k +2 = h +3k -3 ⇒ 2=-3 (impossible), or h +3k +2= -(h +3k -3)

Second case: h +3k +2= -h -3k +3

Bring like terms together:

h +3k +2 +h +3k =3 ⇒ 2h +6k +2=3 ⇒ 2h+6k=1⇒ Divide both sides by 2: h + 3k = 0.5.

So relation between h and k is h=0.5 -3k.

Now, also the points A(-2,0) and B(3,0) lie on circle:

For point A(-2,0):

(h +2)^2 + (k -0)^2=r²

Similarly for point B(3,0):

(h -3)^2 + k^2 = r²

So we have two equations:

1. (h + 2)^2 + k^2= r²
2. (h -3)^2 + k^2= r²

Subtract equation 1 from equation 2:

[(h-3)^2 +k^2] - [(h+2)^2 +k^2]=0 ⇒ (h² -6h +9) - (h² +4h +4)=0 ⇒ (-6h +9)-(4h +4)=0⇒

Combine like terms: (-10h +5)=0 ⇒ -10h= -5 ⇒ h= 0.5.

From previous relation, h =0.5 -3k, so:

0.5=0.5 -3k ⇒ Subtract 0.5 both sides: 0= -3k⇒ k=0

Therefore center is at (h,k)=(0.5,0).

Radius can be found from equation of point A(-2,0):

r²=(0.5 +2)^2 +0^2 = (2.5)^2=6.25

Thus circle has equation: (x - 0.5)^2 + y^2= (2.5)^2.

To solve the given system of equations involving a sphere and other geometric shapes, we need to consider different cases based on which equations are being solved together. Here's an organized summary:

1. **Intersection of Sphere \(x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 4\) with Cylinder \(x^2 + y^2 = 4\)**:
   - Substituting the cylinder equation into the sphere gives \(4 + z^2 = 4\), leading to \(z = 0\).
   - **Result**: The intersection is a circle in the xy-plane at \(z = 0\) with radius 2.

2. **Intersection of Sphere \(x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 4\) with Plane \(z^2 = 1\)**:
   - Substituting \(z^2 = 1\) into the sphere equation gives \(x^2 + y^2 = 3\).
   - **Result**: The intersection consists of two circles in the planes \(z = 1\) and \(z = -1\), each with radius \(\sqrt{3}\).

3. **System Involving Sphere, Cylinder (\(x^2 + y^2 = 4\)), and Plane (\(z^2 = 1\))**:
   - Substituting both equations into the sphere equation results in \(4 + 1 = 5\), which contradicts the sphere's radius.
   - **Result**: No solutions exist for this combined system.

Depending on which set of equations is intended, the solution varies. For clarity, it's essential to specify which equations are being solved together. If further assistance is needed or if there’s a specific case you’d like to explore in detail, feel free to ask!

   \[

   \]

To find the distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we use the formula for the distance from a point to a line:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Here, \(A = 5\), \(B = -6\), and \(C = -28\). Substituting the coordinates of the point into the formula:

1. Calculate the numerator:
   \[
   |5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|
   \]

2. Calculate the denominator:
   \[
   \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]

Thus, the distance is:

\[
\frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} = \frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61}
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x – 6y –28 = 0 is \(\frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61}\).

First, I need to determine the distance between two parallel lines. The given equations are both linear and have identical coefficients for x and y, which confirms they are parallel.

The formula for calculating the distance (d) between two parallel lines Ax + By + C1 = 0 and Ax + By + C2 = 0 is:

d = |C1 - C2| / sqrt(A² + B²)

In this case, A is 3, B is -4, C1 is 12, and C2 is 7.

Plugging these values into the formula:

d = |12 - 7| / sqrt(3² + (-4)²)
d = 5 / sqrt(9 + 16)
d = 5 / sqrt(25)
d = 5 / 5
d = 1

Thus, the distance between the two parallel lines is 1 unit.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

#### 1. Understand the Formula:
The distance \( d \) between two parallel lines of the form \( Ax + By + C_1 = 0 \) and \( Ax + By + C_2 = 0 \) is given by:

\[
d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

#### 2. Identify Coefficients:
From the given equations:
- \( A = 3 \)
- \( B = -4 \)
- \( C_1 = 12 \)
- \( C_2 = 7 \)

#### 3. Plug Values into the Formula:

\[
d = \frac{|12 - 7|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{9 + 16}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{25}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: "1"}}

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Coordinates:** Given points A(4,5), B(6,1), and C(3,4).
2. **Calculate Slope of BC:**
   - Use the formula for slope between two points:
     \( m_{BC} = (y_2 - y_1)/(x_2 - x_1) = (4-1)/(3-6) = 3/-3 = -1 \)
3. **Determine Slope of Altitude:**
   - The altitude is perpendicular to BC, so its slope \( m_{altitude} = -1/m_{BC} = 1 \).
4. **Find Equation of Altitude from A(4,5):**
   - Using point-slope form:
     \( y - 5 = 1(x - 4) \)
     Simplify to get \( y = x + 1 \).
5. **Equation of Line BC:**
   - With slope -1 and passing through B(6,1):
     \( y - 1 = -1(x - 6) \)
     Simplify to \( y = -x +7 \).
6. **Find Foot of Altitude (Intersection Point):**
   - Set equations equal:
     \( x + 1 = -x +7 \Rightarrow 2x=6 \Rightarrow x=3, y=4 \). Thus, the foot is point C(3,4).
7. **Calculate Length of Altitude:**
   - Distance between A(4,5) and C(3,4):
     \( \sqrt{(3-4)^2 + (4-5)^2} = \sqrt{1+1} = \sqrt{2} \).

**Answer:**  
The equation of the altitude is \( y = x + 1 \), and its length is \( \boxed{\sqrt{2}} \).

First, I need to find the midpoint between the two given points (5, 6) and (1, 4). The midpoint is calculated by averaging the x-coordinates and y-coordinates separately.

Next, I'll determine the slope of the line segment connecting these two points. Using the formula for slope, m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1), I can find the initial slope.

After obtaining the slope of the original line, I'll find the slope of the right bisector by taking the negative reciprocal of this value. This ensures that the bisector is perpendicular to the original line.

With the midpoint and the slope of the bisector known, I can use the point-slope form to write the equation of the bisector in its final form.
</think>

To find the equation of the right bisector (perpendicular bisector) of the line segment joining the points \((5, 6)\) and \((1, 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint
The midpoint \(M\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
M = \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope of the Original Line
The slope \(m\) between two points is calculated as:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = \frac{1}{2}
\]

### Step 3: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Bisector
The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope. Thus:
\[
m_{\text{perp}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}} = -2
\]

### Step 4: Write the Equation of the Perpendicular Bisector
Using the point-slope form \(y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)\) with the midpoint \((3, 5)\):
\[
y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
\]
Simplifying:
\[
y - 5 = -2x + 6
\]
\[
y = -2x + 11
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the right bisector is:
\[
\boxed{y = -2x + 11}
\]

The problem requires finding the value of \( h \) such that the line through points \((h, 5)\) and \((6, 3)\) intersects the given line at a right angle. 

1. **Calculate the slope (\( m_1 \)) of the first line:**
   - Points: \((h, 5)\) and \((6, 3)\)
   - Slope formula: \( m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \)
   - \( m_1 = \frac{3 - 5}{6 - h} = \frac{-2}{6 - h} = \frac{2}{h - 6} \)

2. **Determine the slope (\( m_2 \)) of the given line:**
   - Given equation: \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \)
   - Convert to slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)):
     - \( -9y = -7x + 19 \)
     - \( y = \frac{7}{9}x - \frac{19}{9} \)
   - Slope, \( m_2 = \frac{7}{9} \)

3. **Since the lines are perpendicular:**
   - The product of their slopes is \( -1 \): 
     - \( m_1 \times m_2 = -1 \)
   - Substitute the slopes:
     - \( \frac{2}{h - 6} \times \frac{7}{9} = -1 \)

4. **Solve for \( h \):**
   - Cross-multiplied: \( 2 \times 7 = (-1) \times (h - 6) \times 9 \)
   - Simplify: \( 14 = -9(h - 6) \)
   - Divide both sides by -9: 
     - \( h - 6 = -\frac{14}{9} \)
   - Add 6 to both sides:
     - \( h = 6 - \frac{14}{9} = \frac{54}{9} - \frac{14}{9} = \frac{40}{9} \)

**Final Answer:**  
The value of \( h \) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{40}{9}}\).

To find the equation of a line that is perpendicular to the given line \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \) and has an x-intercept at 5, I start by determining the slope of the original line.

First, I'll rewrite the given equation in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)) to identify its slope. Starting with:
\[ x - 7y + 5 = 0 \]
I'll solve for \( y \):
\[
-7y = -x - 5 \\
y = \frac{1}{7}x + \frac{5}{7}
\]
So, the slope (\( m \)) of the original line is \( \frac{1}{7} \).

Since the perpendicular line has a slope that is the negative reciprocal of the original slope, I'll calculate:
\[ m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -7 \]

Now that I have the slope of the desired perpendicular line, I can use the point-slope form to find its equation. The x-intercept is at (5, 0), which gives me a point on the line.

Using the point-slope formula:
\[
y - y_1 = m_{\text{perpendicular}}(x - x_1) \\
y - 0 = -7(x - 5)
\]
Simplifying this equation:
\[
y = -7x + 35
\]

\[

\]
</think>

To find the equation of a line that is perpendicular to the given line \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \) and has an x-intercept at 5, we can follow these steps:

1. **Determine the slope of the original line:**

   Start by rewriting the given equation in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)) to identify its slope.

   \[
   x - 7y + 5 = 0
   \]
   
   Subtract \( x \) and add 5 to both sides:
   \[
   -7y = -x - 5
   \]
   
   Divide all terms by -7:
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{7}x + \frac{5}{7}
   \]
   
   So, the slope (\( m \)) of the original line is \( \frac{1}{7} \).

2. **Find the slope of the perpendicular line:**

   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of its slope.
   
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -7
   \]

3. **Use the point-slope form to find the equation of the perpendicular line:**

   We know that the perpendicular line has an x-intercept at 5, which means it passes through the point (5, 0).

   Using the point-slope formula:
   
   \[
   y - y_1 = m_{\text{perpendicular}}(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Plug in \( m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -7 \) and the point (5, 0):
   
   \[
   y - 0 = -7(x - 5)
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   
   \[
   y = -7x + 35
   \]

4. **Present the equation in standard form:**

   The final equation can be written as:
   
   \[
   7x + y = 35
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
{Final Answer: "7x + y = 35"}

The equation of the line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through (-4, 5) is found by first determining the slope of the original line. \[

To find the distance between the two parallel lines given by the equations \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\) and \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\), we can follow these steps:

1. **Recognize that the lines are parallel**: The coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) in both equations are scalar multiples, confirming they are parallel.

2. **Scale one equation to match the other**: The second equation is twice the coefficients of the first equation:
   \[
   38x + 24y + 70 = 0 \implies 2(19x) + 2(12y) + 70 = 0
   \]

3. **Simplify using a common factor**: The second line can be written as \(2(19x + 12y) + 70 = 0\).

4. **Calculate the distance using the formula for parallel lines**:
   - For two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\), the distance is given by \(\frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}\).
   - Here, the first line is \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\) (where \(C_1 = -38\)) and the second line can be written as \(19x + 12y + \frac{70}{2} = 0\) (since scaling down by 2 gives \(19x + 12y + 35 = 0\) where \(C_2 = 35\)).

5. **Compute the distance**:
   - The difference in constants is \(|-38 - 35| = 73\).
   - The denominator is \(\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2} = \sqrt{361 + 144} = \sqrt{505}\).

Thus, the distance between the two lines is:
\[
\frac{73}{\sqrt{505}}
\]

To rationalize the denominator, we get:
\[
\frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}
\]

### Final Answer
The distance between the two lines is \boxed{\dfrac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}}.

To reduce the equation \(12x - 9y = 18\) into intercept form and find their intercepts on the axes, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Reduce the Equation to Intercept Form
The intercept form of a linear equation is given by:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]
where \(a\) is the x-intercept and \(b\) is the y-intercept.

Starting with the equation:
\[
12x - 9y = 18
\]

**Step 1.1:** Divide both sides of the equation by 18 to make the right side equal to 1.
\[
\frac{12x}{18} - \frac{9y}{18} = \frac{18}{18}
\]
Simplify:
\[
\frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1
\]

**Step 1.2:** Rearrange the equation to match the intercept form.
\[
\frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1
\]
This is now in intercept form.

### Step 2: Identify the Intercepts
From the intercept form:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]

- **x-intercept (\(a\))**: \(\frac{3}{2}\) or \(1.5\)
- **y-intercept (\(b\))**: \(-2\)

### Final Answer
The intercepts on the axes are:
\[
\boxed{\text{X-intercept: } \left( \frac{3}{2}, 0 \right), \text{ Y-intercept: } (0, -2)}
\]

To find the minimal distance from the point (1, 2) to the line given by the equation \(4x + 7y + 5 = 0\), we use the formula for the distance from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to a line \(ax + by + c = 0\):

\[
d = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Here, the coefficients are:
- \(a = 4\)
- \(b = 7\)
- \(c = 5\)

Substituting the coordinates of the point (1, 2) into the formula:

1. Calculate the numerator: 
   \[
   |4 \cdot 1 + 7 \cdot 2 + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = |23| = 23
   \]

2. Calculate the denominator:
   \[
   \sqrt{4^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{16 + 49} = \sqrt{65}
   \]

Thus, the minimal distance is:

\[
d = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}}
\]

To rationalize the denominator, multiply numerator and denominator by \(\sqrt{65}\):

\[
d = \frac{23 \sqrt{65}}{65}
\]

Therefore, the minimal distance from the point (1, 2) to the line \(4x + 7y + 5 = 0\) is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{23\sqrt{65}}{65}}
\]

2. Our measurement line: y = -x + c

To find where they intersect, set them equal:

From equation 1: y = 4x
Plug into equation 2: 4x = -x + c ⇒ 5x = c ⇒ x = c/5, and then y = 4*(c/5) = 4c/5.

So the point of intersection is (c/5, 4c/5).

Now, since this point lies on our measurement line starting from P(4,1), which has slope -1, we can parametrize it as follows:

Parametric equations for the measurement line: x = 4 + t*cos(theta), y = 1 + t*sin(theta), where theta is 135 degrees.

But cos(135°) is -√2/2 and sin(135°) is √2/2. So:

x = 4 - (√2/2)t
y = 1 + (√2/2)t

We know that this point (x, y) must lie on the given line y = 4x.

So substitute into y = 4x:

1 + (√2/2)t = 4*(4 - (√2/2)t)

Let me compute the right side: 4*(4) = 16 and 4*(-√2/2 t) = -2√2 t

So equation becomes:
1 + (√2/2)t = 16 - 2√2 t

Now, collect like terms:

(√2/2)t + 2√2 t = 16 - 1
Multiply both sides by √2 to eliminate denominators. Wait, let's combine the coefficients first.

Let me write all terms with t on one side and constants on the other:

1 = 16 - ( (√2/2) + 2√2 )t

Compute the coefficient: (√2/2) + 2√2 = √2*(1/2 + 2) = √2*(5/2)

So:
1 = 16 - (5√2/2)t
Rearranged:
(5√2/2)t = 16 -1=15

Thus, t = (15 * 2)/(5√2)= 30/(5√2) = 6/√2 = 3√2.

So the parameter t is 3√2. Now, we can find the coordinates of the intersection point:

x = 4 - (√2/2)(3√2) = 4 - ( (3*2)/2 )= 4 - 3 =1

y = 1 + (√2/2)(3√2)=1 + (3*2)/2 =1 +3=4.

So the intersection point is (1,4).

Now, compute the distance between P(4,1) and (1,4):

Distance formula: sqrt[(4-1)^2 + (1-4)^2] = sqrt[9 + 9]=sqrt(18)=3√2.

Wait, but earlier I found t=3√2. Since our parametric line has parameter t representing the actual distance from P along that direction, then yes, it's 3√2.

But let me double-check: Another way is to compute sqrt[(4-1)^2 + (1-4)^2] = sqrt(9+9)=sqrt(18)=3√2. Yep, same result.

To determine the slope of the line passing through the points (6, -4) and (-2, 8), I'll use the slope formula.

First, identify the coordinates:
x₁ = 6, y₁ = -4
x₂ = -2, y₂ = 8

Next, calculate the difference in y-values (y₂ - y₁):
8 - (-4) equals 12.

Then, calculate the difference in x-values (x₂ - x₁):
-2 - 6 equals -8.

Finally, divide the change in y by the change in x to find the slope:
12 divided by -8 simplifies to -3/2.
</think>

To find the slope of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\), we'll use the slope formula:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Coordinates:**
   - Let \((x_1, y_1) = (6, -4)\)
   - Let \((x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)\)

2. **Calculate the Change in \(y\) (\(Δy\)):**
   \[
   Δy = y_2 - y_1 = 8 - (-4) = 8 + 4 = 12
   \]

3. **Calculate the Change in \(x\) (\(Δx\)):**
   \[
   Δx = x_2 - x_1 = -2 - 6 = -8
   \]

4. **Compute the Slope (\(m\)):**
   \[
   m = \frac{Δy}{Δx} = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The slope of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\) is:

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{3}{2}}
\]

To find the slope of a line that makes an inclination of \( 120^\circ \) with the positive direction of the x-axis, we can use trigonometry. The slope \( m \) of a line is given by:

\[
m = \tan(\theta)
\]

where \( \theta \) is the angle of inclination.

Given:
\[
\theta = 120^\circ
\]

So,
\[
m = \tan(120^\circ)
\]

Since \( 120^\circ \) is in the second quadrant where tangent values are negative, and using the reference angle:

\tan(120^\circ) = -\sqrt{3}

The problem involves finding the slope of a second line when the angle between two lines is π/4 radians (or 45 degrees) and one of the slopes is known to be 1/4.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Formula for Angle Between Two Lines:**
   
   The formula relating the angle θ between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is:
   
   \[
   \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
   \]
   
   Given that the angle θ is π/4, we know that \( \tan(\pi/4) = 1 \).

2. **Substitute Known Values:**
   
   Let \( m_1 = \frac{1}{4} \). Plugging into the formula:
   
   \[
   1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} \right|
   \]
   
3. **Remove Absolute Value by Considering Two Cases:**
   
   The equation splits into two possibilities:
   
   - **Case 1:** \( \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = 1 \)
   - **Case 2:** \( \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = -1 \)

4. **Solve Case 1:**
   
   Multiply both sides by the denominator:
   
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = 1 \cdot \left(1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2\right)
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = 1 + \frac{m_2}{4}
   \]
   
   Rearranging terms:
   
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{m_2}{4} = 1 + \frac{1}{4}
   \]
   
   Simplify further:
   
   \[
   \frac{3}{4} m_2 = \frac{5}{4}
   \]
   
   Multiply both sides by \( \frac{4}{3} \):
   
   \[
   m_2 = \frac{5}{3}
   \]
   
5. **Solve Case 2:**
   
   Again, multiply both sides by the denominator:
   
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = -1 \cdot \left(1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2\right)
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   
   \[
   m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = -1 - \frac{m_2}{4}
   \]
   
   Rearranging terms:
   
   \[
   m_2 + \frac{m_2}{4} = -1 + \frac{1}{4}
   \]
   
   Simplify further:
   
   \[
   \frac{5}{4} m_2 = -\frac{3}{4}
   \]
   
   Multiply both sides by \( \frac{4}{5} \):
   
   \[
   m_2 = -\frac{3}{5}
   \]

6. **Conclusion:**
   
   The two possible slopes for the second line are \( \frac{5}{3} \) and \( -\frac{3}{5} \).

**Final Answer:**
The slope of the other line is either \( \boxed{\frac{5}{3}} \) or \( \boxed{-\frac{3}{5}} \).

To solve the problem, we'll follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope of the first line (through points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\)).**
2. **Determine the slope of the second line that is perpendicular to the first line.**
3. **Use the slope formula to find the unknown point \((x, 26)\) on the second line.**

Let's begin:

---

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope of the First Line**

The slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\):
\[
m = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line**

If two lines are perpendicular, their slopes are negative reciprocals of each other. This means:
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m}
\]

Given \( m = \frac{1}{3} \):
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{3}} = -3
\]

**Step 3: Find the Unknown Point on the Second Line**

We know that the second line passes through the points \((10, 14)\) and \((x, 26)\), and its slope is \( -3 \).

Using the slope formula:
\[
-3 = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10}
\]

Simplify the equation:
\[
-3 = \frac{12}{x - 10}
\]

Multiply both sides by \( x - 10 \):
\[
-3(x - 10) = 12
\]

Distribute the \( -3 \):
\[
-3x + 30 = 12
\]

Subtract \( 30 \) from both sides:
\[
-3x = -18
\]

Divide by \( -3 \):
\[
x = 6
\]

---

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{6}

To determine the slope of the line that passes through the origin and the midpoint of the segment joining points P(2, -2) and B(10, 2), I first need to find the midpoint.

The midpoint formula is given by taking the average of the x-coordinates and the average of the y-coordinates of the two points. Applying this to points P and B:

Midpoint (M) = ((2 + 10)/2, (-2 + 2)/2)  
= (12/2, 0/2)  
= (6, 0)

Next, since the line passes through the origin (0, 0), I can use these two points—(0, 0) and (6, 0)—to calculate the slope. The formula for slope (m) is:

m = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁)

Substituting the values:
m = (0 - 0)/(6 - 0)  
= 0/6  
= 0

Therefore, the slope of the line is 0.

To find the slope of a line that passes through the origin and the midpoint of the segment joining points \( P(2, -2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint of Segment PB
The midpoint formula is:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the coordinates of \( P \) and \( B \):
\[
M = \left( \frac{2 + 10}{2}, \frac{-2 + 2}{2} \right) = (6, 0)
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope Between the Origin (0, 0) and Midpoint (6, 0)
The slope formula between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the origin \( (0, 0) \) and midpoint \( (6, 0) \):
\[
m = \frac{0 - 0}{6 - 0} = \frac{0}{6} = 0
\]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\text{Slope of the line is } 0}
\]

To determine the slope of the line that forms a 60-degree angle with the positive direction of the y-axis, I start by identifying the relationship between angles and slopes.

First, since the angle is measured from the positive y-axis in the anticlockwise direction, it's complementary to the standard angle made with the positive x-axis. Therefore, the angle θ with respect to the x-axis is 90° minus 60°, which equals 30°.

Next, I use the tangent function to find the slope (m) of the line because the slope is equal to the tangent of the angle made with the positive x-axis. Calculating tan(30°) gives me a slope of √3/3.
</think>

To determine the slope of the line that forms an angle of \(60^\circ\) with the positive direction of the y-axis measured anticlockwise, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Angle with Respect to the x-Axis:**
   
   - The angle given is measured from the positive y-axis.
   - To find the corresponding angle (\(\theta\)) with respect to the positive x-axis:
     \[
     \theta = 90^\circ - 60^\circ = 30^\circ
     \]

2. **Calculate the Slope:**
   
   - The slope (\(m\)) of a line is equal to the tangent of the angle it makes with the positive x-axis:
     \[
     m = \tan(\theta)
     \]
   - Substituting \(\theta = 30^\circ\):
     \[
     m = \tan(30^\circ) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}
     \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{3}}
\]

Given the problem, we need to find the slopes of lines such that each pair consists of two lines with slopes in a ratio of 1:2. 

We start by considering the equation for the angle between two lines:

\[
\tan \theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

Given that the tangent of the angle is \(\frac{1}{2}\), we have:

\[
\frac{1}{2} = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + m(2m)} \right| = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
\left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right| = \frac{1}{2}
\]

Solving this equation, we get:

\[
\frac{|m|}{1 + 2m^2} = \frac{1}{2}
\]

Cross-multiplying and squaring both sides if necessary, we obtain the solutions for \(m\):

\[
|m| = 1 \quad \text{or} \quad |m| = \frac{1}{2}
\]

Thus, the possible values for \(m\) are:

\[
m = \pm 1 \quad \text{and} \quad m = \pm \frac{1}{2}
\]

Each value of \(m\) corresponds to a pair of slopes where one slope is twice the other. Therefore, the pairs of slopes are:

- For \(m = 1\), the pair is (1, 2).
- For \(m = -1\), the pair is (-1, -2).
- For \(m = \frac{1}{2}\), the pair is (\(\frac{1}{2}\), 1).
- For \(m = -\frac{1}{2}\), the pair is (-\(\frac{1}{2}\), -1).

Thus, the slopes of the lines are:

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{2} \text{ and } 1}, \boxed{-\frac{1}{2} \text{ and } -1}, \boxed{1 \text{ and } 2}, \boxed{-1 \text{ and } -2}
\]

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to find the equations of the lines that are parallel to the axes and pass through the point (0, 5). Hmm, let me think about how to approach this.

First off, I remember that in coordinate geometry, there are two main types of axes: the x-axis and the y-axis. Lines that are parallel to these axes will have specific characteristics in their equations.

Let's start with a line parallel to the x-axis. If a line is parallel to the x-axis, it means it has the same slope as the x-axis itself. Since the x-axis is horizontal, its slope is zero. Therefore, any line parallel to it must also be horizontal and have a slope of zero.

Now, for such a line passing through (0, 5), I recall that the y-coordinate remains constant for all points on this line because it's horizontal. So, if the line passes through (0, 5), every point on this line will have a y-value of 5. That means the equation should be straightforward: y = 5.

Wait, let me double-check. The general form for a horizontal line is indeed y = k, where k is the constant y-value. Since our point is (0, 5), substituting x and y into this formula gives y = 5 regardless of x. That makes sense because no matter what x is, y will always be 5.

To find the equation of the line passing through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7), we first determine the slope \( m \):

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

Using the point-slope form with one of the points, for example, (3, 1):

\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \\
y - 1 = 3(x - 3) \\
y = 3x - 9 + 1 \\
y = 3x - 8
\]

Thus, the equation of the line is \( y = 3x - 8 \).

{Final Answer: "y = 3x - 8"}

To determine if the three points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are collinear, we can use the concept that if any two lines formed by these points have the same slope, then all three points lie on the same straight line.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Calculate the Slope Between Two Pairs of Points:**

   - **First Pair:** \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\)
     \[
     \text{Slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
     \]
   
   - **Second Pair:** \((-2, -2)\) and \((8, 2)\)
     \[
     \text{Slope} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{8 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{10} = \frac{2}{5}
     \]

2. **Compare the Slopes:**

   Since both slopes are equal (\(\frac{2}{5}\)), this indicates that all three points lie on the same straight line.

### Conclusion:

All three points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are collinear because they lie on the same straight line.

4k x + h y =5 h k

The problem involves finding the equation of a line such that the perpendicular from the origin meets it at (2, 13). 

First, determine the slope of the line connecting the origin to the point (2, 13), which is \( \frac{13 - 0}{2 - 0} = \frac{13}{2} \).

The slope of the desired line, being perpendicular, is the negative reciprocal: \( m = -\frac{2}{13} \).

Using the point-slope form with the point (2, 13):

\[ y - 13 = -\frac{2}{13}(x - 2) \]

Simplifying this gives:

\[ y = -\frac{2}{13}x + \frac{4}{13} + 13 \]
\[ y = -\frac{2}{13}x + \frac{173}{13} \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the line is \( y = -\frac{2}{13}x + \frac{173}{13} \).

The line cuts off equal intercepts on both axes, meaning its x-intercept (a) equals its y-intercept (b). Therefore, it can be represented as:

x/a + y/a = 1  
Simplifying, we get:  
x + y = a  

Since the line passes through (4,5), substituting these coordinates into the equation gives:  
4 + 5 = a => a = 9  

Thus, the equation of the line is:  
x + y = 9

Given points \( A(0,0) \) and \( B(2,3) \), we need to find the intercept \( c \) on the y-axis such that the distance from \( A \) to point \( P \) (where \( P \) is the intersection of line \( BP \) with the y-axis) equals this intercept. 

1. The equation of line \( AB \) is \( y = \frac{3}{2}x \).
2. The slope of line \( BP \) is given as \( -\frac{1}{3} \). Using point-slope form, the equation of line \( BP \) passing through \( B(2,3) \) is:
   \[
   y - 3 = -\frac{1}{3}(x - 2)
   \]
   Simplifying this, we get:
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{3}x + \frac{11}{3}
   \]
3. The y-intercept of this line is \( c = \frac{11}{3} \).
4. Point \( P \) where this line intersects the y-axis is \( (0, \frac{11}{3}) \).
5. The distance from \( A(0,0) \) to \( P(0, \frac{11}{3}) \) is:
   \[
   \sqrt{(0 - 0)^2 + \left(\frac{11}{3} - 0\right)^2} = \frac{11}{3}
   \]

Thus, the value of \( c \) is \boxed{\dfrac{11}{3}}.

To find the **equation of the median** from vertex \( R \) in triangle \( PQR \), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Midpoint of Side PQ:**
   
   The vertices are:
   - \( P(2, 1) \)
   - \( Q(-2, 3) \)

   The midpoint \( M \) of side \( PQ \) is calculated as:
   \[
   M\left(\frac{2 + (-2)}{2}, \frac{1 + 3}{2}\right) = M(0,\ 2)
   \]

2. **Determine the Slope of the Median RM:**
   
   The median connects \( R(4,5) \) to midpoint \( M(0,2) \).
   
   The slope \( m \) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{5 - 2}{4 - 0} = \frac{3}{4}
   \]

3. **Write the Equation of the Median:**
   
   Using the point-slope form with point \( R(4,5) \):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{3}{4}(x - 4)
   \]
   
   Simplify to slope-intercept form:
   \[
   y = \frac{3}{4}x + 2
   \]

First, I recognize that to find the distance from a point to a line, I can use the distance formula specific to linear equations.

The given point is (5, -7), and the equation of the line is 5x – 6y –28 = 0.

I'll identify the coefficients: A = 5, B = -6, and C = -28. The coordinates of the point are x₀ = 5 and y₀ = -7.

Next, I'll substitute these values into the distance formula:

Distance (d) = |A*x₀ + B*y₀ + C| / sqrt(A² + B²)

Calculating the numerator: |5*5 + (-6)*(-7) – 28| = |25 + 42 – 28| = |39|

Then, calculating the denominator: sqrt(5² + (-6)^2) = sqrt(25 + 36) = sqrt(61)

Finally, dividing the numerator by the denominator gives the distance.

So, the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line is 39 divided by the square root of 61.

To find the **distance** from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line:

\[
d = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coefficients**:
   - The equation of the line is \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\).
   - Here, \(A = 5\), \(B = -6\), and \(C = -28\).
   
2. **Substitute the coordinates of the point** \((x_0, y_0) = (5, -7)\):
   \[
   d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
   \]

3. **Calculate the numerator**:
   \[
   |5 \times 5 + (-6) \times (-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|
   \]
   
4. **Calculate the denominator**:
   \[
   \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]
   
5. **Compute the distance**:
   \[
   d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
   \]

So, the distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\) is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{39}{\sqrt{61}}}
\]

To find the **right bisector** (perpendicular bisector) of the line segment joining the points \( (5, 6) \) and \( (1, 4) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint
The midpoint \( M \) of the segment is calculated as:
\[
M = \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope of the Original Line
The slope \( m \) between the two points is:
\[
m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = \frac{1}{2}
\]

### Step 3: Determine the Perpendicular Slope
The slope of the perpendicular bisector \( m_{\perp} \) is the negative reciprocal of \( m \):
\[
m_{\perp} = -\frac{1}{m} = -2
\]

### Step 4: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation
Using the midpoint \( (3, 5) \) and the slope \( -2 \), apply the point-slope formula:
\[
y - y_1 = m_{\perp}(x - x_1)
\]
\[
y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
\]

### Step 5: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form
Simplify the equation:
\[
y - 5 = -2x + 6
\]
\[
y = -2x + 11
\]

To find the value of \( h \) such that the line passing through the points \((h, 5)\) and \((6, 3)\) intersects the line \(7x - 9y - 19 = 0\) at a right angle, we follow these steps:

1. **Determine the slope of the given line**:
   The given line is \(7x - 9y - 19 = 0\). Rewriting it in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
   \[
   -9y = -7x + 19 \implies y = \frac{7}{9}x - \frac{19}{9}
   \]
   Thus, the slope \(m_1\) is \(\frac{7}{9}\).

2. **Find the slope of the perpendicular line**:
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another with slope \(m_1\) is the negative reciprocal:
   \[
   m_2 = -\frac{1}{m_1} = -\frac{9}{7}
   \]

3. **Calculate the slope between the two points**:
   The line through \((h, 5)\) and \((6, 3)\) has a slope \(m\) given by:
   \[
   m = \frac{3 - 5}{6 - h} = \frac{-2}{6 - h}
   \]
   
4. **Set the slopes equal and solve for \(h\)**:
   Since \(m_2 = -\frac{9}{7}\), set the two expressions for slope equal:
   \[
   \frac{-2}{6 - h} = -\frac{9}{7}
   \]
   
5. **Cross-multiplied to solve**:
   Cross-multiplying both sides:
   \[
   (-2) \times 7 = (-9) \times (6 - h)
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   -14 = -54 + 9h
   \]
   
6. **Solve for \(h\)**:
   Add 54 to both sides:
   \[
   40 = 9h
   \]
   Divide by 9:
   \[
   h = \frac{40}{9}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The value of \( h \) is \( \boxed{\dfrac{40}{9}} \).

Okay, so I need to find the angle between two lines given by the equations sqrt(3)x + y = 1 and x + sqrt(3)y = 1. Hmm, I remember that when dealing with angles between lines, it's useful to first find their slopes because the angle formula involves the slopes of the two lines.

Let me start by rewriting each equation in slope-intercept form (y = mx + b) so I can easily identify the slopes.

For the first line: sqrt(3)x + y = 1.
I'll solve for y:
y = -sqrt(3)x + 1
So, the slope (m1) is -sqrt(3).

For the second line: x + sqrt(3)y = 1.
Solving for y:
sqrt(3)y = -x + 1
Divide both sides by sqrt(3):
y = (-1/sqrt(3))x + 1/sqrt(3)
So, the slope (m2) is -1/sqrt(3).

tan(theta) = |(m2 - m1)/(1 + m1*m2)|

-1/sqrt(3) + sqrt(3)
