So that's (-1 + 3)/sqrt(3) = 2/sqrt(3)

Denominator:
1 + (sqrt(3)*(1/sqrt(3)))
Simplify inside the parentheses: sqrt(3)*1/sqrt(3) = 1
So, denominator becomes 1 + 1 = 2

Now, tan(theta) is |(2/sqrt(3))/2| = |(1)/sqrt(3)| = 1/sqrt(3)

I know that tan(theta) = 1/sqrt(3), so theta must be the arctangent of 1/sqrt(3). From trigonometry, I remember that tan(30°) = 1/sqrt(3), so theta is 30 degrees. Since angles between lines are always acute or obtuse but we take the smallest angle, which in this case is 30 degrees.

Wait, let me just confirm if there's a possibility of getting an obtuse angle instead. Sometimes when tan(theta) gives you an acute value, it might also correspond to an obtuse angle depending on the configuration, but since both lines have negative slopes, I think they intersect at an acute angle here. Alternatively, sometimes people take theta as the smallest positive angle between them, so 30 degrees should be correct.

I can cross-check by calculating using vectors or dot product if needed, but this seems consistent. The formula gave me tan(theta) = 1/sqrt(3), which corresponds to a 30-degree angle.
</think>

The angle between the lines is calculated using their slopes and the tangent formula for angles between two lines.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Rewrite Equations in Slope-Intercept Form:**
   - For sqrt(3)x + y = 1:
     Solving for y gives y = -sqrt(3)x + 1.
     So, slope m₁ = -sqrt(3).
   
   - For x + sqrt(3)y = 1:
     Solving for y gives y = (-1/sqrt(3))x + (1/sqrt(3)).
     So, slope m₂ = -1/sqrt(3).

2. **Apply the Formula for Angle Between Two Lines:**
   The formula is tan(theta) = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|.

3. **Plug in Slopes into the Formula:**
   tan(theta) = |[(-1/sqrt(3)) - (-sqrt(3))] / [1 + (-sqrt(3))*(-1/sqrt(3))]|

4. **Simplify Numerator and Denominator:**
   - Numerator:
     (-1/sqrt(3)) + sqrt(3) = (2*sqrt(3))/3
   - Denominator:
     1 + [sqrt(3)*(1/sqrt(3))] = 1 + 1 = 2

5. **Compute tan(theta):**
   tan(theta) = |(2*sqrt(3)/3)| / 2| = (2*sqrt(3)/3) / 2 = sqrt(3)/3 ≈ 0.577.

6. **Find theta:**
   arctan(sqrt(3)/3) = 30 degrees.

**Answer:** {Final Answer: "30 degrees"}

Since parallel lines have the same slope, the new line will also have a slope equal to that of the original line. I'll start by converting the original equation into slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)) to determine its slope.

Starting with \( 5x - 6y + 4 = 0 \), I'll solve for \( y \):
\[
-6y = -5x - 4
\]
Dividing both sides by \(-6\) gives:
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
\]
So, the slope (\( m \)) is \( \frac{5}{6} \).

Now, using the point-slope form of a line equation with the point \((-4, 5)\) and slope \( \frac{5}{6} \):
\[
y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
\]
Simplifying this:
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{20}{6} + 5
\]
To combine the constants, convert \( 5 \) to sixths: \( 5 = \frac{30}{6} \).
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{20}{6} + \frac{30}{6}
\]
Adding the fractions:
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{50}{6}
\]
Simplifying \( \frac{50}{6} \) to lowest terms gives \( \frac{25}{3} \).

Therefore, the equation of the parallel line in slope-intercept form is:
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}
\]
To express this without fractions, I'll multiply every term by 6 to eliminate denominators:
\[
6y = 5x + 50
\]
Finally, rearranging it into standard form (\( Ax + By + C = 0 \)):
\[
5x - 6y + 50 = 0
\]

To find the equation of a line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Slope of the Given Line

First, convert the given line into slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)) to find its slope.

Starting with:
\[
5x - 6y + 4 = 0
\]
Subtract \(5x\) and add \(6y\) to both sides:
\[
-6y = -5x - 4
\]
Divide every term by \(-6\):
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
\]
So, the slope (\(m\)) of the given line is:
\[
m = \frac{5}{6}
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Parallel Line

Since parallel lines have the same slope, the new line will also have a slope of \( \frac{5}{6} \).

Use the point-slope form of a linear equation:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Plugging in the point \((-4, 5)\) and the slope \(m = \frac{5}{6}\):
\[
y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
\]

### Step 3: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form

Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate fractions:
\[
6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4)
\]
Simplify:
\[
6y - 30 = 5x + 20
\]
Rearrange terms to isolate \( y \):
\[
6y = 5x + 20 + 30
\]
\[
6y = 5x + 50
\]
Divide all terms by 6:
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}
\]

### Step 4: Final Equation in Standard Form

To express the equation in standard form (\(Ax + By + C = 0\)), rearrange the slope-intercept form:

Starting from:
\[
y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}
\]
Subtract \( y \) from both sides:
\[
\frac{5}{6}x - y + \frac{25}{3} = 0
\]
Multiply every term by 6 to eliminate fractions:
\[
5x - 6y + 50 = 0
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through \((-4, 5)\) is:

\[
\boxed{5x - 6y + 50 = 0}
\]

The problem requires finding all points on the x-axis whose distance from the line \( \frac{x}{3} + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \) is exactly 4 units. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Points on X-Axis:** All points on the x-axis are of the form (h, 0).

2. **Convert Line Equation to Standard Form:** 
   - Starting with \( \frac{x}{3} + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \)
   - Multiply both sides by 12: \( 4x + 3y = 12 \) → \( 4x + 3y - 12 = 0 \)

3. **Apply Distance Formula:** 
   - The distance from (h, 0) to the line is given by:
     \[
     \frac{|4h + 3(0) - 12|}{\sqrt{4^2 + 3^2}} = \frac{|4h - 12|}{5}
     \]
   - Set this equal to 4: \( \frac{|4h - 12|}{5} = 4 \)

4. **Solve for h:** 
   - Multiply both sides by 5: \( |4h - 12| = 20 \)
   - Break into two cases:
     - Case 1: \( 4h - 12 = 20 \) → \( 4h = 32 \) → \( h = 8 \)
     - Case 2: \( 4h - 12 = -20 \) → \( 4h = -8 \) → \( h = -2 \)

5. **Final Points:** The points are (8, 0) and (-2, 0).

**Answer:** {Final Answer: "Points on x-axis are (8, 0) and (-2, 0)}"}

To determine the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), I'll follow these steps:

First, I need to rewrite the equation of the line in standard form. Starting with:
\[ 24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4) \]

Expanding both sides gives:
\[ 24x + 288 = 10y - 40 \]

Next, I'll move all terms to one side to get the standard form \(Ax + By + C = 0\):
\[ 24x - 10y + 328 = 0 \]

Now that the equation is in standard form, I can use the distance formula for a point from a line:
\[ d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Substituting \( A = 24 \), \( B = -10 \), and \( C = 328 \) along with the point \((-2, 2)\):
\[ d = \frac{|24(-2) + (-10)(2) + 328|}{\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2}} \]

Calculating each part step by step:
- \( 24 \times -2 = -48 \)
- \( -10 \times 2 = -20 \)
- Adding these to 328: \( |-48 - 20 + 328| = |260| = 260 \)

For the denominator:
\[ \sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2} = \sqrt{576 + 100} = \sqrt{676} = 26 \]

Finally, dividing numerator by denominator:
\[ d = \frac{260}{26} = 10 \]
</think>

To find the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), we'll use the formula for the distance from a point to a line.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Rewrite the Line Equation in Standard Form:**
   
   Start with the given equation:
   \[
   24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)
   \]
   
   Expand both sides:
   \[
   24x + 288 = 10y - 40
   \]
   
   Rearrange to get all terms on one side (standard form \(Ax + By + C = 0\)):
   \[
   24x - 10y + 328 = 0
   \]
   
2. **Identify Coefficients:**
   
   From the standard form:
   \[
   A = 24,\quad B = -10,\quad C = 328
   \]
   
3. **Use the Distance Formula:**
   
   The distance \(d\) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is given by:
   \[
   d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]
   
4. **Plug in the Values:**
   
   Substituting \(x_0 = -2\) and \(y_0 = 2\):
   \[
   d = \frac{|24(-2) + (-10)(2) + 328|}{\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2}}
   \]
   
5. **Calculate the Numerator:**
   
   Compute each term:
   \[
   24 \times -2 = -48
   \]
   \[
   -10 \times 2 = -20
   \]
   \[
   |-48 - 20 + 328| = |260| = 260
   \]
   
6. **Calculate the Denominator:**
   
   Compute the square roots:
   \[
   24^2 = 576,\quad (-10)^2 = 100
   \]
   \[
   \sqrt{576 + 100} = \sqrt{676} = 26
   \]
   
7. **Find the Distance:**
   
   Divide numerator by denominator:
   \[
   d = \frac{260}{26} = 10
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

```json
{
  "Final Answer": 10
}
```

### Step 1: Check if the Lines are Parallel or Coinciding
First, determine whether the lines are parallel or coinciding by comparing their slopes.

- **First Line:** \(3x + 4y = 9\)  
  Convert to slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
  \[
  4y = -3x + 9 \\
  y = -\frac{3}{4}x + \frac{9}{4}
  \]
  Slope (\(m_1\)) is \(-\frac{3}{4}\).

- **Second Line:** \(6x + 8y = 18\)

  y = -\frac{3}{4}x + \frac{9}{4}
  \]
  Slope (\(m_2\)) is also \(-\frac{3}{4}\).

Since both slopes are equal, the lines are either parallel or coinciding.

- Multiply the first equation by 2 to align coefficients with the second:
  \[
  6x + 8y = 18 \\
  6x + 8y = 18
  \]
  
The modified first equation matches the second, indicating that both equations represent the **same line**.

The line that is equidistant from the lines \( y = x + 2 \) and \( y = x + 6 \) can be found by averaging their constants when expressed in standard form. 

1. Rewrite both lines in standard form:
   - Line 1: \( x - y - 2 = 0 \)
   - Line 2: \( x - y - 6 = 0 \)

2. Average the constants (coefficients of the constant term) from both equations:
   \[
   \frac{-2 + (-6)}{2} = \frac{-8}{2} = -4
   \]

3. The equidistant line is then:
   \[
   x - y - 4 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad y = x + 4
   \]

Thus, the desired line is:

\(\boxed{y = x + 4}\)
